Especially with scheduling. I’ll put an appointment in, and then I’ll go back and look at it again, sometimes more than once, just to make sure I didn’t put the wrong time or the wrong name.\n\nI’m still getting through the day, I mean, I’m there, I’m doing the job, but it takes me longer now because I keep double-checking myself. And then with things like the lobby displays—this is silly, but we have those seasonal notices and little arrangements up front, and I’ll lose track of what I was doing because I’m thinking, “Did I enter that right?” Then I’ve had to redo parts of it because I can’t remember how I meant to set it up.\n\nSo, yes. I check again. Probably more than I need to.\n\nDoctor: When you go back to check something, is it mainly to make the uneasy feeling go down?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… or at least I’m trying to get that feeling to settle. Like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second it’s like, okay, good.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay put. The thought comes back around, and then it’s not just that one thing anymore, it’s, “Well, what if I missed another detail?” And then I’m checking it in my head again, and then something else, and it sort of spreads.\n\nSo yes, I guess the checking is partly to calm that uneasy feeling down, but sometimes it doesn’t calm it for very long.\n\nDoctor: When you check something again, do you feel like you have to keep checking until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “just right,” exactly. It’s more like… I want it to stop poking at me, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look once and then still feel kind of unsure, so I look again, but I don’t know that I’m waiting for a perfect feeling. It’s not like arranging my desk—though I do have a little system with the pens at the front counter, and if someone moves them it throws me off for a second—but with the appointment stuff, it’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it as “until it feels just right.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to arrange or straighten things repeatedly, even when you know it’s unnecessary?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really “driven,” I guess. I do straighten things sometimes, like at the front desk, because people leave brochures all crooked or the pens wander off, and it makes the area look messy. And then someone will come in asking about parking validation, and I’ll realize I’m still lining up the little cards instead of answering the phone, which is embarrassing.\n\nBut repeatedly, like I can’t stop? I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… I like things to look presentable, especially because the lobby is the first thing people see.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, is it because you’re afraid something bad will happen if you don’t check?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. Not always like some huge disaster, but I get this feeling that if I don’t check, some little mistake could turn into something bigger.\n\nLike with the schedule at work, I’ll think, “What if I put the wrong time down and then the whole day gets messed up?” Or bills, paperwork, travel plans… even small things. I know it sounds ordinary, but my mind kind of treats it like one mix-up could set off a chain of problems.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other things too, like whether I remembered to update something or wrote a number correctly, and I sort of lose the original point. So yes, I guess the checking is because I’m worried something bad could happen, just usually in that “small error becomes a big problem” way.\n\nDoctor: When you try not to check, does it feel like you can’t resist doing it anyway?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t really leave. It keeps looping around.\n\nAnd then, before I know it, I’m not even on the first thing anymore—I’m thinking about some other detail, and then another one, and I’m checking it in my head over and over. Like I’ll try to move on, answer the phone or whatever, and part of me is still back there going, “Are you sure?” So… I can resist sometimes, but it doesn’t feel easy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your repeated checking is unreasonable or excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. Part of me knows it’s probably more than I need to do. Like, I’ll check a schedule or a bill and think, okay, that should be enough. But then my mind goes to, what if that one little error causes a bigger mess?\n\nIt’s not even just one thing anymore. It can be work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes… all these ordinary things. And I’ll be thinking about a form at work, and then suddenly I’m remembering the time my cousin missed a flight because of some confusing email—well, that wasn’t even my situation, but my brain uses it like evidence, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I do feel it’s excessive sometimes. I just don’t always feel like it’s unreasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Before you check again, do you usually get the same specific thought that you made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not always the exact same thought, no. It’s more like the same kind of worry.\n\nLike I’ll think, “What if I missed something?” or “What if that number was wrong?” But then it spreads out—work schedules, bills, travel plans, paperwork, all those little everyday things. I’ll start with one small concern, and then my mind treats it like, well, if I made one tiny mistake, it could snowball into a much bigger problem.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a straight yes or no. It’s not always one specific thought, but it’s usually that general fear that some ordinary mix-up is going to cause trouble.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

It’s not always one specific thought, but it’s usually that general fear that some ordinary mix-up is going to cause trouble.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these thoughts ever feel absurd or not really your own, even while they keep coming back?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 44 / receptionist_or_information_clerk / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call them absurd, exactly. They feel… annoying, and too much, but not like they’re someone else’s thoughts or anything.

I mean, they’re definitely my thoughts—my brain is the one making the list and going, “Wait, did you check that?” It’s just that it can get stuck. Like when the front desk gets busy and three people are asking me things at once, I’ll later wonder if I wrote one message down wrong, and then I’m replaying it. But it doesn’t feel unreal or like it came from outside me. Just… hard to shut off, I guess.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F42\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, um… I guess what finally made me come in is I’ve been losing my place at work. Like, I’ll be scheduling appointments and suddenly I’m not sure if I clicked the right time or wrote down the right thing, because my mind kind of slips off to what I might’ve missed. Same thing with the lobby displays—though honestly those displays are always a little fussier than people think, especially when the flyers don’t line up right—but I’ll realize I’ve drifted and have to backtrack. It’s not huge, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly checking your work because you can’t shake the feeling that you might have made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already looked at it,” but it doesn’t really stick. The thought just keeps looping around, like what if I put the wrong time, or missed one little detail—then somehow I’m thinking about three other things I might’ve done wrong too. And then I’m checking it in my head over and over.\n\nSometimes I try to move on, like answer the phone or straighten the brochures—people always leave them half-crooked, which drives me a little nuts—but the worry is still kind of running in the background.\n\nDoctor: When the thought that you made a mistake pops up, does it feel intrusive and hard to dismiss even when you know it probably isn’t true?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does feel hard to shake off. Like I can tell myself, “Sunny, you already checked that, it’s handled,” and for a second I almost believe it, but then it sort of comes back around anyway. And then I’m not just thinking about that one appointment or that one detail, I’m thinking, well, what if I missed something else too?\n\nIt’s like my brain starts hopping from one concern to the next, and pretty soon I’m checking little things in my head over and over. I know it probably isn’t as big a deal as it feels, but it doesn’t really let go easily.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing anything over and over, like rechecking or arranging things, to try to relieve the worry?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure. I mean, I do like things to be in order, like at the desk, because otherwise it just looks bad when people walk in—there’s this one stack of forms that always ends up sliding sideways, and I swear I fix it ten times a day just because it’s in front of me.\n\nBut… as far as doing something over and over to make the worry go away, I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it that way. Sometimes I’ll look back at something or straighten something, but I’m not sure if that counts or if it’s just part of the job.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts feel unwanted and out of character for you, like they intrude even when you don’t want to think about them?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say “out of character,” exactly. I mean… I don’t want to be thinking about it, sure, but I also kind of feel like that’s just me being careful, you know?\n\nLike at work, if something feels off, it’ll keep tugging at me, but then I start thinking about whether the waiting room looks presentable, or whether I forgot to tell someone something, and then—sorry, I’m kind of wandering. I guess I’m not sure what counts as intrusive versus just… my mind not settling down.\n\nDoctor: When these worries come up, do they feel excessive to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, in the moment it feels like I’m just being responsible, but when I say it out loud, it does seem like too much sometimes.\n\nLike, I’ll worry about the work schedule, or whether I paid a bill, or if travel plans are going to get messed up—even little paperwork things, like one wrong date on a form. And then my mind goes, well, what if that turns into a whole bigger problem for someone? Which sounds kind of dramatic, I know.\n\nSorry, I’m thinking of this one stack of appointment sheets at the front desk—but yes, I guess the worries do feel excessive compared to what’s actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about many different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it’s definitely not just work. Work is the easiest one to point to because I’m there all day, but I worry about regular stuff too—bills, travel plans, whether I wrote something down wrong, little paperwork mistakes.\n\nLike, one tiny mix-up and my brain turns it into this whole chain of things that could go wrong. I’ll be thinking about a date on a form, and then suddenly I’m thinking about someone missing an appointment, or a payment getting messed up, or—sorry, I know you asked “not just work.” Yes, it’s a lot of different ordinary things.\n\nDoctor: Have these worries been happening on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… most days, I’d say. It’s been about nine months now, I think.\n\nIt really started after that overnight detention and the paperwork mix-up around it. Since then it’s like my brain keeps going back to forms and details and whether something is going to turn into a problem. I mean, not every single minute, but most days, yes.\n\nDoctor: Aside from that overnight detention and paperwork mix-up, would you still be worrying this much?\n\nPatient: I think… yes. I mean, that was what set it off in my mind, or at least what I keep connecting it to, but it’s not only about that one thing anymore.\n\nNow I’ll worry about all kinds of ordinary things—like the work schedule, or bills, or travel plans, or some tiny paperwork error. It’s like one small mix-up feels like it could turn into this whole bigger problem. Even when I know, logically, it probably won’t.\n\nSorry, I was just thinking about how I double-check dates on forms now—but yes, I think I’d still be worrying this much, because it’s spread into a lot of everyday stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or keyed up when the worrying is happening?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Not like I’m pacing all over the place necessarily, but I feel… wound tight inside.\n\nAt the front desk I can still do the smile and “Hi, how can I help you?” thing, you know, because that’s the job. People come in and they don’t need to know I’m worrying about some little detail. But inside it feels like my shoulders are up around my ears, like I’m bracing for something to go wrong. Sometimes I notice I’m sitting there just kind of… waiting for the next problem, even if nothing is actually happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to control the worrying once it starts?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s probably the part that bothers me most. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” like, I’ll literally say that in my head, and for a second it feels like maybe I’ve put it down.\n\nBut then the thought just kind of loops back around. And then it turns into another thing, like if one detail was off, maybe another detail is off too, and pretty soon I’m mentally checking everything over and over.\n\nIt’s not even always useful checking, it’s just… hard to stop once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: When you worry that you may have made a mistake, do you go back and check the appointment, form, or bill again?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. Especially with scheduling. I’ll put an appointment in, and then I’ll go back and look at it again, sometimes more than once, just to make sure I didn’t put the wrong time or the wrong name.\n\nI’m still getting through the day, I mean, I’m there, I’m doing the job, but it takes me longer now because I keep double-checking myself. And then with things like the lobby displays—this is silly, but we have those seasonal notices and little arrangements up front, and I’ll lose track of what I was doing because I’m thinking, “Did I enter that right?” Then I’ve had to redo parts of it because I can’t remember how I meant to set it up.\n\nSo, yes. I check again. Probably more than I need to.\n\nDoctor: When you go back to check something, is it mainly to make the uneasy feeling go down?\n\nPatient: I think so, yes… or at least I’m trying to get that feeling to settle. Like I’ll tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled,” and for a second it’s like, okay, good.\n\nBut then it doesn’t really stay put. The thought comes back around, and then it’s not just that one thing anymore, it’s, “Well, what if I missed another detail?” And then I’m checking it in my head again, and then something else, and it sort of spreads.\n\nSo yes, I guess the checking is partly to calm that uneasy feeling down, but sometimes it doesn’t calm it for very long.\n\nDoctor: When you check something again, do you feel like you have to keep checking until it feels just right?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “just right,” exactly. It’s more like… I want it to stop poking at me, if that makes sense.\n\nSometimes I’ll look once and then still feel kind of unsure, so I look again, but I don’t know that I’m waiting for a perfect feeling. It’s not like arranging my desk—though I do have a little system with the pens at the front counter, and if someone moves them it throws me off for a second—but with the appointment stuff, it’s more that I’m trying to reassure myself I didn’t miss something.\n\nSo… maybe? I don’t know. I haven’t really thought of it as “until it feels just right.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to arrange or straighten things repeatedly, even when you know it’s unnecessary?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not really “driven,” I guess. I do straighten things sometimes, like at the front desk, because people leave brochures all crooked or the pens wander off, and it makes the area look messy. And then someone will come in asking about parking validation, and I’ll realize I’m still lining up the little cards instead of answering the phone, which is embarrassing.\n\nBut repeatedly, like I can’t stop? I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… I like things to look presentable, especially because the lobby is the first thing people see.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, is it because you’re afraid something bad will happen if you don’t check?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. Not always like some huge disaster, but I get this feeling that if I don’t check, some little mistake could turn into something bigger.\n\nLike with the schedule at work, I’ll think, “What if I put the wrong time down and then the whole day gets messed up?” Or bills, paperwork, travel plans… even small things. I know it sounds ordinary, but my mind kind of treats it like one mix-up could set off a chain of problems.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other things too, like whether I remembered to update something or wrote a number correctly, and I sort of lose the original point. So yes, I guess the checking is because I’m worried something bad could happen, just usually in that “small error becomes a big problem” way.\n\nDoctor: When you try not to check, does it feel like you can’t resist doing it anyway?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t really leave. It keeps looping around.\n\nAnd then, before I know it, I’m not even on the first thing anymore—I’m thinking about some other detail, and then another one, and I’m checking it in my head over and over. Like I’ll try to move on, answer the phone or whatever, and part of me is still back there going, “Are you sure?” So… I can resist sometimes, but it doesn’t feel easy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your repeated checking is unreasonable or excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. Part of me knows it’s probably more than I need to do. Like, I’ll check a schedule or a bill and think, okay, that should be enough. But then my mind goes to, what if that one little error causes a bigger mess?\n\nIt’s not even just one thing anymore. It can be work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes… all these ordinary things. And I’ll be thinking about a form at work, and then suddenly I’m remembering the time my cousin missed a flight because of some confusing email—well, that wasn’t even my situation, but my brain uses it like evidence, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I do feel it’s excessive sometimes. I just don’t always feel like it’s unreasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Before you check again, do you usually get the same specific thought that you made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not always the exact same thought, no. It’s more like the same kind of worry.\n\nLike I’ll think, “What if I missed something?” or “What if that number was wrong?” But then it spreads out—work schedules, bills, travel plans, paperwork, all those little everyday things. I’ll start with one small concern, and then my mind treats it like, well, if I made one tiny mistake, it could snowball into a much bigger problem.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a straight yes or no. It’s not always one specific thought, but it’s usually that general fear that some ordinary mix-up is going to cause trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel absurd or not really your own, even while they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them absurd, exactly. They feel… annoying, and too much, but not like they’re someone else’s thoughts or anything.\n\nI mean, they’re definitely my thoughts—my brain is the one making the list and going, “Wait, did you check that?” It’s just that it can get stuck. Like when the front desk gets busy and three people are asking me things at once, I’ll later wonder if I wrote one message down wrong, and then I’m replaying it. But it doesn’t feel unreal or like it came from outside me. Just… hard to shut off, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

And then someone will come in asking about parking validation, and I’ll realize I’m still lining up the little cards instead of answering the phone, which is embarrassing.\n\nBut repeatedly, like I can’t stop? I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed it that way. It’s more just… I like things to look presentable, especially because the lobby is the first thing people see.\n\nDoctor: When you feel the need to check something again, is it because you’re afraid something bad will happen if you don’t check?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s part of it. Not always like some huge disaster, but I get this feeling that if I don’t check, some little mistake could turn into something bigger.\n\nLike with the schedule at work, I’ll think, “What if I put the wrong time down and then the whole day gets messed up?” Or bills, paperwork, travel plans… even small things. I know it sounds ordinary, but my mind kind of treats it like one mix-up could set off a chain of problems.\n\nAnd then I start thinking about other things too, like whether I remembered to update something or wrote a number correctly, and I sort of lose the original point. So yes, I guess the checking is because I’m worried something bad could happen, just usually in that “small error becomes a big problem” way.\n\nDoctor: When you try not to check, does it feel like you can’t resist doing it anyway?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I can tell myself, “Sunny, it’s handled, you already looked,” but the thought doesn’t really leave. It keeps looping around.\n\nAnd then, before I know it, I’m not even on the first thing anymore—I’m thinking about some other detail, and then another one, and I’m checking it in my head over and over. Like I’ll try to move on, answer the phone or whatever, and part of me is still back there going, “Are you sure?” So… I can resist sometimes, but it doesn’t feel easy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your repeated checking is unreasonable or excessive?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes and no. Part of me knows it’s probably more than I need to do. Like, I’ll check a schedule or a bill and think, okay, that should be enough. But then my mind goes to, what if that one little error causes a bigger mess?\n\nIt’s not even just one thing anymore. It can be work schedules, bills, travel plans, little paperwork mistakes… all these ordinary things. And I’ll be thinking about a form at work, and then suddenly I’m remembering the time my cousin missed a flight because of some confusing email—well, that wasn’t even my situation, but my brain uses it like evidence, you know?\n\nSo… I guess I do feel it’s excessive sometimes. I just don’t always feel like it’s unreasonable in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Before you check again, do you usually get the same specific thought that you made a mistake?\n\nPatient: Not always the exact same thought, no. It’s more like the same kind of worry.\n\nLike I’ll think, “What if I missed something?” or “What if that number was wrong?” But then it spreads out—work schedules, bills, travel plans, paperwork, all those little everyday things. I’ll start with one small concern, and then my mind treats it like, well, if I made one tiny mistake, it could snowball into a much bigger problem.\n\nSorry, I know that’s not a straight yes or no. It’s not always one specific thought, but it’s usually that general fear that some ordinary mix-up is going to cause trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts ever feel absurd or not really your own, even while they keep coming back?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them absurd, exactly. They feel… annoying, and too much, but not like they’re someone else’s thoughts or anything.\n\nI mean, they’re definitely my thoughts—my brain is the one making the list and going, “Wait, did you check that?” It’s just that it can get stuck. Like when the front desk gets busy and three people are asking me things at once, I’ll later wonder if I wrote one message down wrong, and then I’m replaying it. But it doesn’t feel unreal or like it came from outside me. Just… hard to shut off, I guess."
